Otherwise, print the exchange rate of currency you all know against the burle for tomorrow. This should be the maximum possible number of those that are even and that are obtained from today's exchange rate by swapping exactly two digits. Exchange rate representation should not contain leading zeroes.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have today's exchange rate `n` which is an odd positive integer
2. Tomorrow's exchange rate will be:
   - An even number (so it must end with an even digit)
   - Obtained by swapping exactly two distinct digits in today's number
   - The maximum possible such number
3. If no such number exists, return -1

## Key Observations

1. Since tomorrow's rate must be even, the last digit must be even (0, 2, 4, 6, 8)
2. Today's rate is odd, so its last digit is odd (1, 3, 5, 7, 9)
3. To make the number even, we need to swap the last digit (odd) with some other digit that is even
4. We want the maximum possible number, so we should try to maximize the digits from left to right
5. We can only swap exactly two distinct digits

## Strategy

1. Convert the input number to a string to work with individual digits
2. We need to find a position `i` (from 0 to len-2) where digit `digits[i]` is even, and swap it with the last digit
3. For each possible even digit position (not the last position), try swapping with the last digit
4. Among all valid swaps, choose the one that produces the maximum number
5. We need to ensure no leading zeros in the result

## Step-by-step approach:

1. Convert the number to string
2. Iterate through all possible positions `i` from 0 to len-2
3. For each position `i` where `digits[i]` is even:
   - Swap digits[i] and digits[-1]
   - Check if the resulting number has no leading zeros
   - Keep track of the maximum valid result
4. If no valid swap produces an even number, return -1

## Algorithm:

3. For each such position, create a new number by swapping digits[i] and digits[-1]

Actually, no - because the digit at each position is determined by the swap. The value of the number is determined by the digits in each position.

For each possible swap position i (where digit[i] is even), we create a new number by:
- Putting digit[-1] at position i
- Putting digit[i] at the end

## Implementation:

```python
n = input().strip()
digits = list(n)
len_n = len(digits)

# Find all positions where digit is even (and not at the last position)
even_positions = []
for i in range(len_n - 1):  # from 0 to len-2
    if int(digits[i]) % 2 == 0:
        even_positions.append(i)

# If no even digit found in the first len-1 positions, impossible
if not even_positions:
    print(-1)
else:
    candidates = []
    for pos in even_positions:
        # Create a new digit list by swapping pos with last position
        new_digits = digits[:]
        new_digits[pos] = digits[-1]
        new_digits[-1] = digits[pos]
        
        # Convert to string and check for leading zeros
        new_num = ''.join(new_digits)
        if new_num[0] == '0' and len_n > 1:  # leading zero and not single digit
            continue
            
        candidates.append(new_num)
    
    # If no valid candidate found
    if not candidates:
        print(-1)
    else:
        # Find the maximum candidate
        print(max(candidates))
```

So algorithm:
1. Convert input to list of digits
2. Find all positions where digit is even
3. If no even digits exist, return -1
4. For each even digit at position j, try to swap it with every other position i (i ≠ j)
5. After swap, check if the resulting number has an even last digit
6. Check if the number has leading zeros (first digit is 0)
7. Among all valid results, keep the maximum

But this is O(n²) which might be too slow for 10^5 digits.

Wait, constraint says length from 2 to 10^5. 10^5 digits, O(n²) would be 10^10 operations, which is too slow in Python.

We need a more efficient approach.

Let me think again: can we avoid checking all pairs?

Key insight for efficiency:
- We only care about swaps that produce an even last digit
- For the last digit to be even, either:
  - The last digit was even originally (so j = len(n)-1 and we swap it with some i ≠ len(n)-1)
  - The last digit was odd originally, and we swap it with some j where digit[j] is even

But in the last case, we swap position len(n)-1 with some position j where digit[j] is even.

So the valid swaps are:
1. Swap an even digit at position j (where j ≠ len(n)-1) with position len(n)-1
2. Swap an even digit at position j with some other position i (where i ≠ j and i ≠ len(n)-1)

Wait, actually no - when we swap position j and i, the last digit becomes digit[i] if i = len(n)-1, or digit[j] if j = len(n)-1.

So the last digit becomes even if:
- We swap j and len(n)-1, and digit[j] is even, OR
- We swap j and i where i ≠ j, and j = len(n)-1 (so last digit becomes digit[i])

So for the last digit to be even, either:
- j = len(n)-1 and digit[j] is even, and we swap j with some i
- j ≠ len(n)-1 and digit[j] is even, and we swap j with len(n)-1

Actually, that's not right. When we swap positions j and i:
- If i = len(n)-1, then after swap, digit at i becomes digit[j], so if digit[j] is even, then last digit becomes even
- If j = len(n)-1, then after swap, digit at j becomes digit[i], so if digit[i] is even, then last digit becomes even

So actually, for any swap between j and i, the last digit becomes even if either:
- j = len(n)-1 and digit[i] is even, or
- i = len(n)-1 and digit[j] is even

But since we're swapping two digits, one of them must be at the last position.

Therefore, the only possible swaps that create an even last digit are:
1. Swap position len(n)-1 with some position j where digit[j] is even
2. Swap position len(n)-1 with some position j where digit[j] is odd (but this would make last digit = digit[j], which is odd - only if digit[j] is even)

Wait, let me be precise:
- After swapping positions j and i:
  - If j = len(n)-1, then position j gets digit[i], so for last digit to be even, digit[i] must be even
  - If i = len(n)-1, then position i gets digit[j], so for last digit to be even, digit[j] must be even

So the condition is: digit at the position that is NOT the last one in the swap must be even.

So for a swap between j and i where one of them is the last position, the last digit becomes even if the digit at the non-last position is even.

Therefore, valid swaps are:
- For every position j ≠ len(n)-1 where digit[j] is even, swap j and len(n)-1
- For every pair (i,j) where i ≠ j and i ≠ len(n)-1 and j ≠ len(n)-1, swap i and j if digit[i] or digit[j] is even? 

No. When we swap i and j (both not last), the last digit remains unchanged. Since original last digit is odd (given), the new last digit is still odd. So this swap does NOT create an even last digit.

Therefore, the only swaps that create an even last digit are swaps involving the last position with some other position j where at the time of swap, the digit from j is even.

Specifically:
- We swap position len(n)-1 with some position j where digit[j] is even
- After swap, the last digit becomes digit[j] (which is even)
- The digit at position j becomes digit[len(n)-1]

So only swaps with the last position and an even digit at position j (j ≠ len(n)-1) will create an even last digit.

Therefore, algorithm:
1. Convert input to list of digits
2. Find all positions j where 0 ≤ j < len(n)-1 and digit[j] is even
3. If no such positions exist and the last digit is already even, then we can only swap last digit with some other digit, but that would make last digit even only if we swap with an even digit - so we need an even digit somewhere else.
4. Actually, if the last digit is already even, then we can swap it with some position i where digit[i] is odd (so after swap, last digit becomes digit[i] which is odd - not good).
5. Only if we swap last digit with a position j where digit[j] is even, then last digit becomes digit[j] (even).

So in all cases, to make last digit even, we must swap the last digit with some position j where digit[j] is even.

Therefore:
- Find all positions j where 0 ≤ j < len(n)-1 and digit[j] is even
- For each such position j, create a candidate by swapping digit[j] and digit[len(n)-1]
- Among all candidates, find the maximum one
- Also check if the resulting number has leading zeros (first digit ≠ 0)

But what if there are no even digits at all? Then no valid swap exists.

Also, what if we have even digits at positions before the last? We use those.

What about swapping an even digit with a digit at position len(n)-1?

For example, 527: last digit is 7 (odd), even digits at position 1 (digit 2). Swap position 1 and 2: [5,7,2] = 572, which is valid.

For 4573: last digit is 3 (odd), even digits at position 0 (digit 4). Swap position 0 and 3: [3,5,7,4] = 3574.

For 1357997531: no even digits, so return -1.

But is this the maximum possible?

Consider if there's a swap between two non-last positions that somehow makes the last digit even? No, because original last digit is odd, and we're only swapping two digits - if neither position is last, the last digit remains unchanged.

So only swaps involving the last position can make it even.

Therefore, the algorithm is:
1. Read input string n
2. Convert to list of digits
3. Find all positions j (0 ≤ j < len(n)-1) where digit[j] is even
4. If no such positions exist, return -1
5. For each such position j, create candidate by:
   - new_digits = digits[:]
   - swap digits[j] and digits[len(n)-1]
   - form the new number
6. Among all valid candidates, return the maximum one
7. A candidate is invalid if the first digit is 0 (leading zero)

But is there a possibility that swapping with a non-last even digit might not give the maximum?

What if we have multiple even digits? We should try all combinations and pick the maximum.

For example: 1234
- Last digit is 4 (even), so we could swap with any position j < 3 where digit[j] is even
- digit[0]=1 (odd), digit[1]=2 (even), digit[2]=3 (odd)
- So j=1 is even
- Swap j=1 and last: swap 2 and 4 → 1432
- But what if we want to swap with another digit? Only j=1
- Answer should be 1432

But is there a larger even number? What about if we didn't swap last digit? But we need even last digit.

But 1234 already has even last digit, so we can swap it with any other digit. But when we swap last digit with position j, the last digit becomes digit[j].

For example, swap last digit (4) with position 1: 1432
Swap last digit with position 0: 4231 - but when we do this, the last digit becomes digit[0]=1, which is odd - invalid!

Ah! Important point: when we swap position j and the last position, the new last digit is digit[j]. So for this to be even, digit[j] must be even.

So in the algorithm, we only consider positions j where digit[j] is even.

Therefore, only even digits at non-last positions can be used to swap with the last position to create an even last digit.

So the algorithm is correct.

Edge case: leading zero
- After swapping, if the first digit becomes 0, the number has leading zeros, which is invalid.
- For example, if we have "102" and we swap last digit (2) with first digit (1), we get "201" which is valid.
- But if we have "102" and we swap last digit with middle digit (0), we get "102" -> "102" (no change), but if we have "102" and swap middle digit (0) with last digit (2), we get "120", which is valid.
- But if we have "100" and swap middle digit (0) with last digit (0), we get "100" - but this swap swaps two identical digits, and the problem says "exactly two distinct digits", so swaps of identical digits are not allowed?

Wait! The problem says "swapping exactly two distinct digits".

So if two digits are the same, swapping them doesn't change the number, but it's swapping two identical digits.

But the problem says "distinct digits" - are they meant to be different values or different positions?

Let me check: "swapping exactly two distinct digits in it"

I think "distinct" here means two different positions, not necessarily different values.

For example, in 100, swapping positions 1 and 2 (both 0) - are these "distinct digits"? Probably means distinct positions.

And the problem says "exactly two distinct digits" - I think it means we swap two different positions.

So even if the digits are the same, we're still swapping two positions.

But the result would be the same number.

However, the problem says "obtained from the present rate by swapping exactly two distinct digits", and if the digits are the same, the number doesn't change.

But the new number would be the same as original, which is odd, so it wouldn't be even.

So such swaps would not produce an even number anyway.

Therefore, we can ignore swaps between positions with identical digits that don't produce an even last digit.

In our algorithm, for a swap between j and last position to produce an even last digit, we require digit[j] is even. Even if digit[j] == digit[last], the last digit becomes digit[j] which is even, so it's valid.

But the resulting number might be the same as original only if digit[j] == digit[last] and they are both even.

For example, n = "122": last digit is 2 (even). We can swap position 1 and 2 (digits 2,2) → "122" same. This doesn't produce an even number? It does - it's 122, which is even. But we started with 122 which is even, but the problem says today's rate is odd!

Important: today's rate is guaranteed to be odd.

So last digit is odd.

Therefore, the last digit is odd, so when we swap it with position j, the new last digit is digit[j], and since digit[j] is even (our condition), the new number has even last digit.

So the original number has odd last digit, and we're making it even by swapping.

Now, could there be a swap between two non-last positions that makes the last digit even? No, because last digit remains odd.

Therefore, only swaps involving the last position with an even digit at a non-last position work.

But is there a swap that swaps two non-last digits, and after swap, the last digit becomes even? Only if one of the positions is last.

So yes, only swaps involving last position with some j where digit[j] is even will work.

Therefore, algorithm:
1. Read n as string
2. Convert to list of digits (integers)
3. Let len_n = len(n)
4. Find all positions j where 0 ≤ j < len_n-1 and digit[j] is even
5. If no such positions, return -1
6. Generate all candidates by swapping position j and position len_n-1 for each such j
7. For each candidate, convert to string and check if first character is '0' (leading zero)
8. Among valid candidates, find the maximum one
9. Return the maximum candidate

But wait, what if the last digit is already even? The input is guaranteed to be odd, so last digit is odd. So we don't have to worry about that.

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: "527"
- digits: [5,2,7]
- last digit is 7 (odd)
- positions j < 2 where digit[j] is even: j=1 (digit 2 is even)
- swap j=1 and last: [5,7,2] = "572"
- no leading zero, so valid
- return "572"

Example 2: "4573"
- digits: [4,5,7,3]
- last digit is 3 (odd)
- positions j < 3 where digit[j] is even: j=0 (digit 4 is even)
- swap j=0 and last: [3,5,7,4] = "3574"
- no leading zero, so valid
- return "3574"

Example 3: "1357997531"
- digits: [1,3,5,7,9,9,7,5,3,1]
- last digit is 1 (odd)
- are there any even digits at positions 0 to 8? All are odd. So no even digits at non-last positions
- return -1

This matches.

But what about a case where there might be a larger number by swapping in a different way?

Even digits at positions: j=1 (digit 2 is even)

Any other swaps? Only this one.

So answer is 1532.

But what if we have "1223" - digits [1,2,2,3], odd
Even digits at positions: j=1, j=2

Swap j=2 and last: [1,2,3,2] = 1232
Maximum is 1322.

Is there a swap that could give larger? For example, if we swap position 0 and last? Position 0 has digit 1 (odd), so not considered. Only even digits at non-last positions.

So this seems correct.

Even digits: j=1 (digit 0 is even)

Swap j=2 and last: same

But first digit is 1, so no leading zero.

Is there a case where after swap, first digit becomes 0?

Suppose "10001" - we have even digits at positions 1,2,3
Swap j=1 and last: [1,1,0,0,0] = 11000 - valid
Swap j=2 and last: [1,0,1,0,0] = 10100 - valid  
Swap j=3 and last: [1,0,0,1,0] = 10010 - valid
All start with 1.

What if we have "201"? Input is odd, last digit 1
Even digits: j=0 (digit 2 is even)
Swap j=0 and last: [1,0,2] = 102 - valid, starts with 1.

What if "301"? Swap: [1,0,3] = 103 - valid.

Can we get a number starting with 0? Only if first digit becomes 0.

For that, we need to swap the first digit (pos 0) with some other digit.

But in our algorithm, we only swap with the last digit. So when we swap position j and last, position 0 stays in position 0.

So the first digit only changes if we swap position 0 with last.

In that case, if we swap position 0 and last, the new first digit is the old last digit.

But the last digit is odd (original), so if we swap, new first digit = old last digit (odd), so it's never 0.

Therefore, after any such swap, the first digit remains the same as original, unless we swap position 0 with last, in which case it becomes the old last digit (which is odd and cannot be 0 since it's odd and not zero? Wait, odd digits are 1,3,5,7,9, so never 0).

Therefore, the first digit after any swap is either:
- The original first digit (if we don't swap position 0)
- The original last digit (if we swap position 0 with last)

The original last digit is odd, so it's at least 1, so it's never 0.

Therefore, no swap will create a leading zero.

So we don't need to explicitly check for leading zeros.

Therefore, the algorithm is:

```python
n = input().strip()
digits = [int(d) for d in n]
len_n = len(n)

# Find all positions j where 0 <= j < len_n-1 and digits[j] is even
even_positions = []
for j in range(len_n - 1):
    if digits[j] % 2 == 0:
        even_positions.append(j)

if not even_positions:
    print(-1)
else:
    candidates = []
    for j in even_positions:
        # Create copy of digits
        new_digits = digits[:]
        # Swap j and last position
        new_digits[j], new_digits[len_n-1] = new_digits[len_n-1], new_digits[j]
        # Convert to string
        candidate = ''.join(str(d) for d in new_digits)
        candidates.append(candidate)
    
    # Find maximum candidate
    candidates.sort(reverse=True)
    print(candidates[0])
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have today's exchange rate `n` which is an odd positive integer
2. Tomorrow's exchange rate will be:
   - An even number (so it must end with an even digit)
   - Obtained by swapping exactly two distinct digits in today's number
   - The maximum possible such number
3. If no such number exists, return -1

## Key Observations

1. Since the result must be even, the last digit must be even (0, 2, 4, 6, 8)
2. We need to swap exactly two distinct digits
3. We want the maximum possible result
4. The original number is odd, so it ends with an odd digit (1, 3, 5, 7, 9)

## Strategy

1. We need to find a valid swap that makes the last digit even
2. Since we want the maximum result, we should:
   - Try to keep as many high digits as possible in their original positions
   - Find the best position to swap with the last digit (or another even digit)

Therefore, my approach is correct.

## Edge Cases

- If there are no even digits in positions 0 to len-2, return -1
- If swapping results in leading zero, that's invalid
- We need to find the maximum result among all valid swaps

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: 527
- Digits: [5,2,7], last digit is 7 (odd)
- Even digits in positions 0,1: position 1 has 2 (even)
- Swap position 1 and position 2: [5,7,2] = 572
- This is valid (no leading zero) and the answer is 572 ✓

Example 2: 4573
- Digits: [4,5,7,3], last digit is 3 (odd)
- Even digits in positions 0,1,2: position 0 has 4 (even)
- Swap position 0 and position 3: [3,5,7,4] = 3574
- This is valid (no leading zero) and the answer is 3574 ✓

Example 3: 1357997531
- This has only odd digits: 1,3,5,7,9,9,7,5,3,1
- Wait, let me check: 1,3,5,7,9,9,7,5,3,1 - all odd!
- So no even digits in the number, so answer should be -1 ✓

But wait, is there an even digit? 1357997531 - no even digits, so indeed -1.

## Implementation

```python
n = input().strip()
digits = list(n)
length = len(digits)

# Find all positions where digit is even and position < length-1
even_positions = []
for i in range(length - 1):  # positions 0 to length-2
    if int(digits[i]) % 2 == 0:
        even_positions.append(i)

if not even_positions:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Try each even position and swap with last digit
    candidates = []
    for pos in even_positions:
        # Create new digit list by swapping pos and last position
        new_digits = digits[:]
        new_digits[pos], new_digits[-1] = new_digits[-1], new_digits[pos]
        
        # Convert to string and check for leading zeros
        new_num = ''.join(new_digits)
        
        # Check if first digit is '0'
        if new_num[0] == '0':
            continue
            
        candidates.append(new_num)
    
    if candidates:
        print(max(candidates))
    else:
        print(-1)
```

Wait, I need to be more careful. Is this the maximum possible? 

In example 2: 4573
- Only even position is 0 (digit 4)
- Swap positions 0 and 3: [3,5,7,4] = 3574
- This is the only candidate, so max is 3574 ✓

But what if we have multiple even digits? Consider a case like 24689:
- Even digits at positions 0,1,2
- Swap 0 and 4: [9,4,6,8,2] = 94682
- Swap 1 and 4: [2,8,6,4,9] = 28649  
- Swap 2 and 4: [2,4,8,6,9] = 24869
- Maximum is 94682

But is there a possibility of getting a larger number by swapping two non-last digits? 

Wait, no. The only way to make the last digit even is to swap it with an even digit from another position. Any other swap would leave the last digit odd.

So our approach is correct.

Let me verify with a more complex example:
n = 2468
Wait, 2468 is even, but the problem states today's rate is odd.

So all inputs are odd.

Final code:

E869120 found a chest which is likely to contain treasure.

One more thing he found is a sheet of paper with the following facts written on it:  
 - Condition 1: The string S contains a string T as a contiguous substring.
 - Condition 2: S is the lexicographically smallest string among the ones that satisfy Condition 1.
Print the string S.

If such a string does not exist, print UNRESTORABLE.  

-----Constraints-----
 - 1 \leq |S'|, |T| \leq 50
 - S' consists of lowercase English letters and ?.
 - T consists of lowercase English letters.

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
S
T'

-----Output-----
Print the string S.

If such a string does not exist, print UNRESTORABLE instead.  

-----Sample Input-----
?tc????
coder

-----Sample Output-----
atcoder

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a string S' with lowercase letters and '?' characters
2. We need to find a string S that:
   - Has the same length as S'
   - Matches S' in positions where S' has letters (S[i] = S'[i] when S'[i] != '?')
   - Contains T as a contiguous substring
   - Is lexicographically smallest among all such strings
3. If no such string exists, output "UNRESTORABLE"

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the constraints
- We need to find the lexicographically smallest string that satisfies:
  - It matches S' where S' has fixed characters
  - It contains T as a contiguous substring
  - All characters are lowercase letters

### Step 2: Strategy
1. First, check if it's possible to form a string that contains T as a substring while matching S'
2. For each possible position where T can be placed in S', check if it's compatible with S'
3. Among all valid positions, find the lexicographically smallest resulting string

### Step 3: Implementation approach
1. Generate all possible valid positions where T could appear in S'
2. For each valid position, construct the string S by:
   - Using S'[i] for positions where S'[i] != '?'
   - For positions where S'[i] == '?', assign the smallest possible character (initially 'a')
   - But ensure that in the positions where T starts, we place the correct characters from T
3. Among all valid constructed strings, pick the lexicographically smallest

### Step 4: Key insights
- The lexicographically smallest string will start with as many 'a's as possible
- We need to ensure that T appears somewhere in the string
- We can try placing T at each possible starting position in S'

Let me implement this:

```python
def solve():
    S_prime = input().strip()
    T = input().strip()
    
    n = len(S_prime)
    m = len(T)
    
    # If T is longer than S', it's impossible
    if m > n:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Try each possible starting position for T in S'
    valid_strings = []
    
    # Try placing T starting at position i (0 to n-m)
    for start_pos in range(n - m + 1):
        # Create a candidate string based on S_prime
        candidate = list(S_prime)
        
        # Mark positions that are fixed by S_prime
        # For positions where S_prime has letters, we must use that letter
        # For positions where S_prime has '?', we can choose
        
        # First, check if placing T starting at 'start_pos' is possible
        valid = True
        
        # Check each position in T
        for i in range(m):
            pos = start_pos + i
            if pos >= n:
                valid = False
                break
                
            if S_prime[pos] != '?':
                if S_prime[pos] != T[i]:
                    valid = False
                    break
        
        if not valid:
            continue
            
        # Now construct the candidate string
        # Use S_prime's characters when they are fixed
        # Use 'a' for positions that are '?', except where T is placed
        for i in range(n):
            if i >= start_pos and i < start_pos + m:
                # This position is part of the T substring
                candidate[i] = T[i - start_pos]
            elif S_prime[i] != '?':
                # This position has a fixed character
                candidate[i] = S_prime[i]
            else:
                # This position is '?', we can set it to 'a' for lexicographically smallest
                candidate[i] = 'a'
        
        # Convert to string and add to valid_strings
        valid_strings.append(''.join(candidate))
    
    # If no valid string found, return UNRESTORABLE
    if not valid_strings:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Return the lexicographically smallest
    valid_strings.sort()
    print(valid_strings[0])

solve()
```

- Position 1: S'[1] = 't' → fixed

One more thing: I need to consider that we might be able to have multiple valid placements, and we need the lexicographically smallest overall string.

But in this case, since we're trying all possible placements and constructing the lexicographically smallest possible string for each placement, and then taking the minimum, it should work.

However, I need to ensure that when I construct the string, I'm making it lexicographically smallest by placing 'a' at all '?' positions that are not constrained by T.

In my current algorithm, I'm setting all '?' positions to 'a' when not part of T. But is that always optimal?

Yes, because setting a position to 'a' (the smallest possible character) makes the string lexicographically smaller than any larger character.

But what if there's a constraint that forces a larger character? No, because if a position is '?' and not part of T, it can safely be set to 'a'.

So my algorithm is correct.

```python
def solve():
    S_prime = input().strip()
    T = input().strip()
    
    n = len(S_prime)
    m = len(T)
    
    # If T is longer than S', it's impossible
    if m > n:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    valid_strings = []
    
    # Try each possible starting position for T in S'
    for start_pos in range(n - m + 1):
        # Check if T can be placed starting at start_pos
        valid = True
        for i in range(m):
            pos = start_pos + i
            if pos >= n:
                valid = False
                break
            if S_prime[pos] != '?' and S_prime[pos] != T[i]:
                valid = False
                break
        
        if not valid:
            continue
        
        # Construct the candidate string
        candidate = list(S_prime)
        
        # Place T in the appropriate positions
        for i in range(m):
            pos = start_pos + i
            candidate[pos] = T[i]
        
        # Fill all other '?' positions with 'a' to make it lexicographically smallest
        for i in range(n):
            if S_prime[i] == '?' and candidate[i] == '?':

        return

    valid_strings.sort()
```

Wait, there's a bug. When I set `candidate[pos] = T[i]`, I'm overriding whatever was in S_prime[pos] if it was a letter. But what if S_prime[pos] was a letter and it didn't match T[i]? I already checked that in the validity condition.

Actually, my validity condition checks that if S_prime[pos] != '?' then it must equal T[i]. So if it doesn't, we skip this placement.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a string S' with some letters replaced by '?'
2. We need to find a string S that:
   - Contains T as a contiguous substring
   - Is lexicographically smallest among all such strings
   - Is compatible with S' (meaning where S' has letters, S must match those letters)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the problem
- We need to find the lexicographically smallest string S that:
  1. Contains T as a contiguous substring
  2. Matches S' in all positions where S' has non-'?' characters
- If no such string exists, return "UNRESTORABLE"

### Step 2: Key insights
- Since we want the lexicographically smallest string, we should try to place T as early as possible and with the smallest possible characters
- For positions where S' has a specific letter, that letter must be in our final string
- For positions with '?', we can choose the smallest possible character (which is 'a')
- However, we also need to ensure that T appears as a contiguous substring

### Step 3: Strategy
1. First, check if it's possible to satisfy the constraints with the given S'
2. Try to find the best position to place T in S'
3. For each possible starting position of T in S', check if it's compatible with S'
4. Among all valid positions, choose the one that gives us the lexicographically smallest result

### Step 4: Implementation approach
- For each possible starting position where T can fit in S' (from 0 to len(S') - len(T)), check if placing T there is compatible with S'
- Compatibly means that for each position in T, if S' has a letter at that position, it must match
- Also ensure that the letters in S' that are not part of T can be filled with 'a'
- Among all valid positions, choose the one that gives the lexicographically smallest result

### Step 5: Edge cases
- T cannot fit in S' at any position → UNRESTORABLE
- Some positions in S' have fixed letters that conflict with T → UNRESTORABLE

Let me implement this:

```python
def solve():
    S_prime = input().strip()
    T = input().strip()
    
    n = len(S_prime)
    m = len(T)
    
    # If T is longer than S', it's impossible
    if m > n:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Try each possible starting position for T in S'
    best_string = None
    
    for start_pos in range(n - m + 1):
        # Check if we can place T starting at start_pos
        compatible = True
        temp_string = list(S_prime)
        
        # Check compatibility with T at position start_pos
        for i in range(m):
            pos_in_S_prime = start_pos + i
            if S_prime[pos_in_S_prime] != '?' and S_prime[pos_in_S_prime] != T[i]:
                compatible = False
                break
        
        if not compatible:
            continue
            
        # Build the string with T placed at start_pos and other positions filled with 'a'
        result = list(S_prime)
        
        # Replace the positions where T is placed with T's characters
        for i in range(m):
            pos_in_S_prime = start_pos + i
            result[pos_in_S_prime] = T[i]
        
        # Fill all other positions with 'a' (since we want lexicographically smallest)
        # But we need to preserve the original letters where S_prime has non-?
        # Actually, we need to be careful - positions that have letters in S_prime should keep those letters
        # But in our construction above, we already handled the T placement
        # For positions that are not part of T, we need to make sure they match S_prime
        # Actually, our temp_string should already be set correctly
        
        # Let's recreate the result properly
        result = []
        for i in range(n):
            if i >= start_pos and i < start_pos + m:
                result.append(T[i - start_pos])
            else:
                if S_prime[i] != '?':
                    result.append(S_prime[i])
                else:
                    result.append('a')
        
        # Check if this result is valid (all fixed positions match)
        valid = True
        for i in range(n):
            if S_prime[i] != '?' and result[i] != S_prime[i]:
                valid = False
                break
        
        if valid:
            if best_string is None or result < best_string:
                best_string = ''.join(result)
    
    if best_string is None:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
    else:
        print(best_string)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a string S' with lowercase letters and '?' characters
2. We need to find a string S that:
   - Matches S' (same length, and when '?' is replaced, it matches S')
   - Contains T as a contiguous substring
   - Is lexicographically smallest among all such strings
3. If no such string exists, output "UNRESTORABLE"

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the constraints
- We need to find the lexicographically smallest string that:
  1. Matches the pattern S' (preserving known letters, replacing '?' with any lowercase letter)
  2. Contains T as a contiguous substring

### Step 2: Key insights
- To get the lexicographically smallest string, we should try to place T as early as possible and use 'a' for all '?' characters when possible
- However, we need to ensure that T appears as a contiguous substring
- If T cannot fit anywhere in the pattern S', or if the pattern constraints make it impossible to satisfy both conditions, we return "UNRESTORABLE"

### Step 3: Algorithm approach
1. First, check if T can fit in positions within S' (considering the constraints of known characters)
2. Try to place T in different possible positions and check if it's feasible
3. Among all feasible placements of T, find the one that produces the lexicographically smallest result
4. For each position where T can be placed, construct the resulting string and choose the lexicographically smallest one

### Step 4: Detailed steps
1. Iterate through all possible starting positions where T could fit in S' (from 0 to len(S') - len(T))
2. For each position i, check if placing T starting at i is valid:
   - For each position j in T, check if S'[i+j] is either '?' or equals T[j]
3. If valid, construct the string by:
   - For positions before i: use the original characters, replacing '?' with 'a' (to minimize)
   - For position i to i+len(T)-1: use T
   - For positions after i+len(T)-1: use the original characters, replacing '?' with 'a'
4. Among all valid constructions, return the lexicographically smallest one

### Step 5: Handle edge cases
- If no valid position for T exists, return "UNRESTORABLE"
- If a constraint forces a letter to be greater than 'a' somewhere, we need to handle that

```python
def solve():
    S_prime = input().strip()
    T = input().strip()
    
    n = len(S_prime)
    m = len(T)
    
    # If T is longer than S_prime, impossible
    if m > n:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Try all possible starting positions for T
    valid_strings = []
    
    for start in range(n - m + 1):
        # Check if T can be placed starting at 'start'
        valid = True
        for j in range(m):
            pos = start + j
            if S_prime[pos] != '?' and S_prime[pos] != T[j]:
                valid = False
                break
        
        if valid:
            # Construct the string with T placed at start
            result = []
            for i in range(n):
                if i < start or i >= start + m:
                    # Not in T substring
                    if S_prime[i] != '?':
                        result.append(S_prime[i])
                    else:
                        result.append('a')  # use smallest possible letter
                else:
                    # In T substring
                    result.append(T[i - start])
            
            valid_strings.append(''.join(result))
    
    # If no valid placement found
    if not valid_strings:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Return lexicographically smallest
    valid_strings.sort()
    print(valid_strings[0])

solve()
```

                break

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a string S' with some characters replaced by '?'
2. We need to find the original string S that:
   - Contains T as a contiguous substring
   - Is lexicographically smallest among all such strings
   - Matches the pattern of S' (where '?' can be replaced with any lowercase letter)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the constraints
- We need to find a string S that:
  - Has T as a contiguous substring
  - Matches the pattern of S' (where '?' can be anything)
  - Is lexicographically smallest

### Step 2: Strategy
Since we want the lexicographically smallest string, we should:
1. First check if it's possible to place T somewhere in the string
2. Try all possible positions where T can be placed in S'
3. For each valid position, fill in the remaining positions with the smallest possible letters (a)
4. Among all valid possibilities, pick the lexicographically smallest result

### Step 3: Implementation approach
1. Iterate through all possible starting positions where T can be placed in S'
2. For each position, check if T fits exactly in that position (matching characters or being '?' in S')
3. For valid placements, construct the resulting string by:
   - Placing T at the chosen position
   - Replacing all '?' with 'a' (since 'a' is the smallest letter)
   - When there's a conflict (a regular character in S' doesn't match the corresponding character in T), skip that position
4. Among all valid constructions, return the lexicographically smallest one

### Step 4: Edge cases
- No valid position exists → return "UNRESTORABLE"
- The string S' might be longer than T, so we need to handle the prefix/suffix properly

1. Try to place T as early as possible (to get lexicographically smaller result)
2. For each position where T can be placed, construct the string
3. Among those valid constructions, pick the lexicographically smallest

So the optimal approach is:
1. Try all possible positions where T can be embedded in S'
2. For each valid embedding, create the resulting string by:
   - At the embedding position, place T exactly
   - For all other positions, if S' has a letter, use that letter; if S' has '?', use 'a'
3. Among all valid results, return the lexicographically smallest one

But we don't need to try all positions - we can be smarter. Since we want lexicographically smallest, we should try to place T as early as possible in a way that doesn't force us to use larger letters later.

Actually, the lexicographic order is determined by the first differing character. So we want to make the leftmost possible positions 'a' whenever possible, as long as we still can satisfy the condition that T appears as a substring.

### Step 5: Algorithm

```python
def solve():
    S_prime = input().strip()
    T = input().strip()
    
    n = len(S_prime)
    m = len(T)
    
    # If T is longer than S', it's impossible
    if m > n:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Try all possible starting positions for T in S_prime
    candidates = []
    
    for start in range(n - m + 1):
        # Check if we can place T starting at position 'start'
        valid = True
        # Check if T fits at position 'start'
        for i in range(m):
            if S_prime[start + i] != '?' and S_prime[start + i] != T[i]:
                valid = False
                break
        
        if valid:
            # Construct the string if valid
            result = list(S_prime)
            # Place T at the starting position
            for i in range(m):
                result[start + i] = T[i]
            
            # For all positions that are not in T, if they are '?', replace with 'a'
            for i in range(n):
                if i >= start and i < start + m:
                    continue  # already set to T[i]
                elif S_prime[i] == '?':
                    result[i] = 'a'
                # If S_prime[i] is a letter, we keep it (but only if it's not conflicting with T)
                # Actually, we already checked that T doesn't conflict with S_prime at position start+i
                # And for other positions, S_prime[i] is either a letter or '?'
            
            # Convert to string and add to candidates
            candidates.append(''.join(result))
    
    # If no valid candidate, return UNRESTORABLE
    if not candidates:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Return the lexicographically smallest candidate
    candidates.sort()
    print(candidates[0])
```

   - If valid, create a candidate string by:

Let me code this:

```python
S_prime = input().strip()
T = input().strip()

n = len(S_prime)
m = len(T)

result_candidates = []

# Try each possible starting position for T
for i in range(n - m + 1):
    # Check if T can start at position i
    valid = True
    for j in range(m):
        if S_prime[i + j] != '?' and S_prime[i + j] != T[j]:
            valid = False
            break
    
    if valid:
        # Create candidate string
        candidate = list(S_prime)
        # Replace all '?' with 'a' initially
        for idx in range(n):
            if candidate[idx] == '?':
                candidate[idx] = 'a'
        
        # Now, place T at position i
        for j in range(m):
            candidate[i + j] = T[j]
        
        result_candidates.append(''.join(candidate))

# If no valid candidates, return UNRESTORABLE
if not result_candidates:
    print("UNRESTORABLE")
else:
    # Find lexicographically smallest candidate
    result_candidates.sort()
    print(result_candidates[0])
```

Wait, this might not be optimal. When we replace all '?' with 'a' first, and then overwrite the T part, we're essentially setting the T part to T, and the rest to 'a'. Actually, no. Because for any position before the start of T, if it's '?', we should set it to 'a' which is the smallest possible. For any position after the end of T, if it's '?', we should set it to 'a' which is the smallest. For positions in T, we have to set them to the characters of T. 

And since we're only allowed to increase characters (from 'a'), setting to 'a' gives the lexicographically smallest result for non-T positions.

Therefore, the candidate construction is correct.

So the algorithm seems correct.

Edge case: when T has length 0? But constraint says |T| >= 1.

One more edge case: when T can't fit in S'? When len(T) > len(S'), then no valid position. In that case, n - m + 1 <= 0, so the for loop won't run, and we return "UNRESTORABLE".

This is correct.

Therefore, final code:

```python
S_prime = input().strip()
T = input().strip()

n = len(S_prime)
m = len(T)

result_candidates = []

# Try each possible starting position for T
for i in range(n - m + 1):
    # Check if T can start at position i
    valid = True
    for j in range(m):
        if S_prime[i + j] != '?' and S_prime[i + j] != T[j]:
            valid = False
            break
    
    if valid:
        # Create candidate string
        candidate = list(S_prime)
        # Replace all '?' with 'a' initially
        for idx in range(n):
            if candidate[idx] == '?':
                candidate[idx] = 'a'
        
        # Now, place T at position i
        for j in range(m):
            candidate[i + j] = T[j]
        
        result_candidates.append(''.join(candidate))

# If no valid candidates, return UNRESTORABLE
if not result_candidates:
    print("UNRESTORABLE")
else:
    # Find lexicographically smallest candidate
    result_candidates.sort()
    print(result_candidates[0])
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a string S' that contains lowercase letters and '?' characters
2. We need to find a string S that:
   - Contains T as a contiguous substring
   - Is lexicographically smallest among all such strings
   - Matches the pattern of S' (meaning letters must match at positions where S' has letters, and '?' can be replaced with any letter)

3. If no such string exists, output "UNRESTORABLE"

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the constraints
- We need to find the lexicographically smallest string that:
  - Matches S' (same letters at positions where S' has letters)
  - Contains T as a contiguous substring

### Step 2: Approach
Since we want the lexicographically smallest string, we should:
1. First, check if it's possible to place T as a substring in S'
2. Try to place T in all possible positions within S'
3. For each possible placement, check if it's compatible with S' (i.e., doesn't conflict with existing letters)
4. Among all valid placements, choose the one that gives lexicographically smallest result
5. For positions where S' has '?', we should fill them with 'a' to get the lexicographically smallest string

### Step 3: Algorithm
1. Generate all possible positions where T can be placed as a substring in S'
2. For each position, check if placing T there doesn't conflict with existing letters in S'
3. If it doesn't conflict, create a candidate string by:
   - Using S' letters where they exist
   - Replacing '?' with 'a' (to minimize lexicographically)
   - Placing T in the specific position
4. Among all valid candidates, return the lexicographically smallest one

### Step 4: Implementation details
- The substring T can start at position i where 0 ≤ i ≤ len(S') - len(T)
- For each starting position, check if placing T there conflicts with S'
- Conflict means at any position j in the substring, if S'[i+j] is a letter and it doesn't equal T[j]
- If no conflict, create the candidate string and keep track of the lexicographically smallest

### Step 5: Edge cases
- If no valid placement exists, return "UNRESTORABLE"
- If T is longer than S', it's impossible

```python
def solve():
    S_prime = input().strip()
    T = input().strip()
    
    n = len(S_prime)
    m = len(T)
    
    # If T is longer than S', impossible to have T as substring
    if m > n:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Try all possible starting positions for T in S'
    candidates = []
    
    for start_pos in range(n - m + 1):
        # Check if we can place T starting at start_pos without conflict
        valid = True
        temp_s = list(S_prime)  # Convert to list for easy modification
        
        # Check if placing T at start_pos conflicts with existing letters
        for j in range(m):
            pos = start_pos + j
            if pos < n:
                if S_prime[pos] != '?' and S_prime[pos] != T[j]:
                    valid = False
                    break
        
        if valid:
            # Create the candidate string by placing T at start_pos
            # First, make a copy of S_prime as list
            candidate = list(S_prime)
            
            # Place T at the starting position
            for j in range(m):
                pos = start_pos + j
                if pos < n:
                    candidate[pos] = T[j]
            
            # Fill all '?' with 'a' to get lexicographically smallest
            for i in range(n):
                if candidate[i] == '?':
                    candidate[i] = 'a'
            
            candidates.append(''.join(candidate))
    
    # If no valid candidate found
    if not candidates:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Return lexicographically smallest candidate
    candidates.sort()
    print(candidates[0])

solve()
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a string S' with some characters replaced by '?'
2. We need to find the original string S that:
   - Contains T as a contiguous substring
   - Is lexicographically smallest among all such strings
3. If no such string exists, output "UNRESTORABLE"

## Key Insights

1. The lexicographically smallest string means we should try to fill the '?' characters with 'a' as much as possible
2. However, we must ensure that T appears as a contiguous substring somewhere in S
3. Since we want lexicographically smallest, we should try to place T in the earliest possible position, and fill the rest with 'a'

## Approach

1. First, check if it's possible to form a string containing T as substring from S'
2. For each possible position where T can be placed in S', check if it's valid (i.e., the characters in S' at those positions either match T or are '?')
3. Among all valid positions, find the one that would produce the lexicographically smallest result
4. To get lexicographically smallest result, we want to:
   - Place T as early as possible (to minimize the string)
   - Fill all other positions with 'a' (since 'a' is the smallest letter)

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Find all valid positions where T can appear** in S'
   - For each starting position i in S' where i + len(T) <= len(S'), check if S'[i:i+len(T)] matches T (with '?' acting as wildcards)
   - If yes, this position is valid

2. **If no valid position exists, return "UNRESTORABLE"**

3. **If valid positions exist, construct the lexicographically smallest string:**
   - Find the leftmost valid position (this gives us the lexicographically smallest result)
   - For all positions in S', if they are in the range of T's placement, use T's characters
   - Otherwise, use 'a'

4. **Wait - is this correct?** Actually, we need to be careful. The position where T appears might not always give the lexicographically smallest result if we can place T such that it allows more 'a's elsewhere.

But actually, for lexicographically smallest, we want to minimize the string from left to right. So:
- We should place T in the earliest possible position where it can fit
- This ensures that we don't have to put a larger character early in the string
- Then fill everything else with 'a'

Let me trace through the example:
- S' = "?tc????", T = "coder"
- We need to place "coder" somewhere in S'
- T has length 5, so we can start at positions 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 (since 4+5=9 > 6, so only 0,1,2,3)
- Position 0: S'[0:5] = "?tc??", should be "coder" → ? must be 'c', t must be 'o' → conflict (t≠o)
- Position 1: S'[1:6] = "tc???" should be "coder" → t must be 'c' → conflict
- Position 2: S'[2:7] = "c????" should be "coder" → c matches, then ??? should match o,d,e,r → ok with ?
- So position 2 works

Wait, let me recalculate: 
- Position 0: S'[0:5] is positions 0,1,2,3,4: "?tc??"
- T is "coder", so we need: 
  - pos0: ? → c
  - pos1: t → o → no match
  - So invalid

- Position 1: S'[1:6] = "tc???" 
  - pos1: t → c → no match
  - invalid

- Position 2: S'[2:7] = "c????" 
  - pos2: c → c → match
  - pos3: ? → o → ok (wildcard)
  - pos4: ? → d → ok
  - pos5: ? → e → ok
  - pos6: ? → r → ok
  - So valid at position 2

- Position 0: ? → we want 'a' to be lexicographically smallest
- Position 1: t → fixed
- Position 2: c → from T
- Position 3: ? → from T → 'o'

So positions for T: can start at 0,1,2 (since 0+5=5, 1+5=6, 2+5=7 > 7? 2+5=7, and we have 7 positions: 0..6, so index 6 exists, so 2+5=7 is out of bounds).

So valid starts: 0,1,2 (since 2+5=7, and indices go from 0 to 6, so max start is 6-5+1 = 2)

- Start at 0: S'[0:5] = "?tc??", need to match "coder"
  - pos0: ? → c → ok
  - pos1: t → o → no (t≠o) → invalid

- Start at 1: S'[1:6] = "tc???", need to match "coder"
  - pos1: t → c → no (t≠c) → invalid

  - pos2: c → c → ok
  - pos3: ? → o → ok
  - pos4: ? → d → ok
  - pos5: ? → e → ok
  - pos6: ? → r → ok → valid

So only position 2 works.

Now construct the result:
- Position 0: ? → can be 'a' (smallest)
- Position 1: t → fixed
- Position 2: c → from T
- Position 3: o → from T
- Position 4: d → from T
- Position 5: e → from T
- Position 6: r → from T

So S = "atcoder"

This matches the expected output.

## Algorithm

1. Read S' and T
2. Find all valid starting positions for T in S' (where T can fit and the characters match with wildcards)
3. If no valid position exists, return "UNRESTORABLE"
4. Otherwise, find the leftmost valid position (this gives lexicographically smallest result)
5. Construct the result string:
   - For each position in S':
     - If it's within the T range, use T's character at that offset
     - Otherwise, if S' has a letter, use that letter; if it's '?', use 'a' (to get smallest lexicographic order)
6. Return the constructed string

Wait - actually, when we have a '?' that's not in T's range, we should use 'a' to get the lexicographically smallest result.

Let me verify with the algorithm:

For each position i in S':
- If i is in [start, start+len(T)-1], use T[i-start]
- Else if S'[i] is a letter, use that letter
- Else (S'[i] is '?'), use 'a'

This ensures lexicographically smallest.

```python
def solve():
    S_prime = input().strip()
    T = input().strip()
    
    n = len(S_prime)
    m = len(T)
    
    # Find all valid starting positions for T in S_prime
    valid_starts = []
    
    for start in range(n - m + 1):
        # Check if T can be placed starting at 'start'
        valid = True
        for j in range(m):
            if S_prime[start + j] != '?' and S_prime[start + j] != T[j]:
                valid = False
                break
        
        if valid:
            valid_starts.append(start)
    
    # If no valid position found, return UNRESTORABLE
    if not valid_starts:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Find the leftmost valid start (gives lexicographically smallest result)
    start_pos = valid_starts[0]
    
    # Construct the result string
    result = []
    for i in range(n):
        if start_pos <= i < start_pos + m:
            # This position is covered by T
            result.append(T[i - start_pos])
        elif S_prime[i] != '?':
            # This position has a fixed character
            result.append(S_prime[i])
        else:
            # This position is '?' and not covered by T, use 'a'
            result.append('a')
    
    print(''.join(result))

solve()
```

def solve():

    T = input().strip()
    
    n = len(S_prime)
    m = len(T)
    
    # Find all valid starting positions for T in S_prime
    valid_starts = []
    
    for start in range(n - m + 1):
        # Check if T can be placed starting at 'start'
        valid = True
        for j in range(m):
            if S_prime[start + j] != '?' and S_prime[start + j] != T[j]:
                valid = False
                break
        
        if valid:
            valid_starts.append(start)
    
    # If no valid position found, return UNRESTORABLE
    if not valid_starts:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Find the leftmost valid start (gives lexicographically smallest result)
    start_pos = valid_starts[0]
    
    # Construct the result string
    result = []
    for i in range(n):
        if start_pos <= i < start_pos + m:
            # This position is covered by T
            result.append(T[i - start_pos])
        elif S_prime[i] != '?':
            # This position has a fixed character
            result.append(S_prime[i])
        else:

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a string S' with some letters replaced by '?'
2. We need to find the original string S that:
   - Contains T as a contiguous substring
   - Is lexicographically smallest among all such strings
   - Must be consistent with S' (i.e., where S' has letters, S must have the same letter)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Identify what we need to find
- We need to reconstruct S from S' by replacing '?' with letters
- S must contain T as a contiguous substring
- S must be lexicographically smallest

### Step 2: Strategy
Since we want the lexicographically smallest string, we should:
1. Try to place T in the string such that it fits within S'
2. For positions where S' has fixed letters, we can't change them
3. For positions with '?', we should try to put the smallest possible letter ('a')
4. However, we must ensure that T appears as a contiguous substring

### Step 3: Algorithm approach
1. First, check if it's possible to place T somewhere in S' to satisfy the constraints
2. For each possible position where T could start (from 0 to len(S') - len(T)), check if placing T there is possible
3. Among all valid placements, choose the one that gives the lexicographically smallest result
4. For positions that are not part of T or where S' has fixed letters, fill with 'a' (smallest letter)

### Step 4: Implementation details
- For each possible starting position i where T can be placed (i from 0 to len(S') - len(T))
- Check if placing T starting at position i would conflict with S' (i.e., at positions i+i to i+len(T)-1, if S'[pos] is not '?' then it must equal T[pos-i])
- If no conflict, construct the string by:
  - Using S'[pos] if it's not '?'
  - Using T[pos-i] if the position is part of T
  - Using 'a' for all other positions
- Among all valid constructions, return the lexicographically smallest one

### Step 5: Handle edge cases
- If no valid placement of T exists, return "UNRESTORABLE"
- Ensure all constraints are satisfied

```python
def solve():
    S_prime = input().strip()
    T = input().strip()
    
    n = len(S_prime)
    m = len(T)
    
    # If T is longer than S_prime, impossible to contain T as substring
    if m > n:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Possible starting positions for T in S_prime
    possible_positions = []
    
    for start in range(n - m + 1):
        # Check if placing T starting at 'start' is valid
        valid = True
        for j in range(m):
            pos = start + j
            if S_prime[pos] != '?' and S_prime[pos] != T[j]:
                valid = False
                break
        
        if valid:
            possible_positions.append(start)
    
    # If no valid position found, return UNRESTORABLE
    if not possible_positions:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Among all valid positions, we want the lexicographically smallest result
    # We'll try each valid position and construct the result
    candidates = []
    
    for start in possible_positions:
        result = list(S_prime)
        
        # Place T at starting position
        for j in range(m):
            pos = start + j
            result[pos] = T[j]
        
        # Fill all remaining '?' with 'a' to make it lexicographically smallest
        for i in range(n):
            if result[i] == '?':
                result[i] = 'a'
        
        candidates.append(''.join(result))
    
    # Return the lexicographically smallest candidate
    candidates.sort()
    print(candidates[0])

solve()
```

Wait, I need to reconsider. The above approach might not be optimal because we're not considering that some positions might be forced to be higher letters due to constraints.

Actually, the approach above is correct because:
1. We ensure that T appears as a substring by placing it at valid starting positions
2. For positions that are fixed in S', we don't change them
3. For positions that are '?' and not part of T, we set them to 'a' to get the lexicographically smallest string
4. We consider all valid placements of T and pick the lexicographically smallest result

Let me verify with the sample:
- S' = "?tc????"
- T = "coder"

Possible starting positions for "coder": 
- 0: positions 0-5: S'[0] = '?' → OK, S'[1] = 't' vs 'c' → not OK
- 1: positions 1-6: S'[1] = 't' vs 'c' → not OK  
- 2: positions 2-7: S'[2] = 'c' vs 'c' → OK, S'[3] = 'o' vs 'o' → OK, S'[4] = 'd' vs 'd' → OK, S'[5] = 'e' vs 'e' → OK, S'[6] = 'r' vs 'r' → OK

Only position 2 works. So we place "coder" starting at position 2:
- Original S': ? t c ? ? ? ?
- After placing "coder" at position 2: ? t c o d e r
- Fill remaining '?' with 'a': ? t c o d e r → only position 0 is '?', so becomes 'a'

Result: "atcoder" ✓

The solution looks correct.

```python
def solve():
    S_prime = input().strip()
    T = input().strip()
    
    n = len(S_prime)
    m = len(T)
    
    # If T is longer than S_prime, impossible to contain T as substring
    if m > n:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Possible starting positions for T in S_prime
    possible_positions = []
    
    for start in range(n - m + 1):
        # Check if placing T starting at 'start' is valid
        valid = True
        for j in range(m):
            pos = start + j
            if S_prime[pos] != '?' and S_prime[pos] != T[j]:
                valid = False
                break
        
        if valid:
            possible_positions.append(start)
    
    # If no valid position found, return UNRESTORABLE
    if not possible_positions:
        print("UNRESTORABLE")
        return
    
    # Among all valid positions, construct the lexicographically smallest result
    best_string = None
    
    for start in possible_positions:
        result = list(S_prime)
        
        # Place T at starting position
        for j in range(m):
            pos = start + j
            result[pos] = T[j]
        
        # Fill all remaining '?' with 'a' to make it lexicographically smallest
        for i in range(n):
            if result[i] == '?':
                result[i] = 'a'
        
        current_string = ''.join(result)
        if best_string is None or current_string < best_string:
            best_string = current_string
    
    print(best_string)

solve()
```
